Document 385: Preview of 2026 New Zealand General Election (2026-11-04)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Preview of 2026 New Zealand General Election (2026-11-04).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-11-04
Pages: 13
Status: ok

### Page 1

1
Westpac New Zealand Economics
• Polling points to a very close and unusually
fragmented election, with the outcome
likely to depend on the performance of the
Opportunity Party and whether any party
achieves “overhang” seats.
• While either Labour or National will lead the
next government, minor parties are likely to
have unprecedented representation in the next
government. This makes it harder to assess
the mix of policies that will emerge from

coalition negotiations.
• Compared to the status quo, a returned
National-led government with stronger
ACT and NZ First representation may be
more aggressive on some combination
of expenditure restraint, welfare and
immigration, resource development
and deregulation.
• A Labour-Green-Opportunity administration
would probably deliver more public-service
and social investment, a broader tax base,
stronger climate and labour regulation and
likely greater borrowing for infrastructure.

A centre-left government may find it hard to
resist pressure to lift public service salaries
and the minimum wage in response to fuel-
driven inflation.
• The fiscal starting point acts as a constraint:
the PREFU projects that OBEGALx will remain
in deficit until 2028/29 and achieving that
outcome will require exceptional spending
control in the current environment.
• Whichever government is elected will need
to make hard trade-offs, or raise additional

revenue or debt. The latter will be costly with
global investors requiring higher interest rates
to fund government (and other) debt.
• A key non-fiscal outcome of a change
in government would likely be the
implementation of the Labour Party’s
proposal to restore the RBNZ’s dual inflation-
employment mandate.
The election appears close, but the bigger
story is fragmentation.
New Zealand goes to the ballot box on 7 November
(although advance voting started on 26 October). The

campaign is taking place against a mixed economic
backdrop: activity has recovered somewhat, but
households remain sensitive to high living costs and a
weak housing market; the fiscal accounts have improved
relative to the Budget but still show deficits until 2028/29.
That combination gives the various parties room to
argue about priorities, but little room to avoid difficult
trade-offs. Additional spending – which may be difficult
to avoid in the current environment – will require either

additional revenue or a willingness to accept additional
debt issuance at a time when international investors are
seeking greater compensation to fund government debt
(and other).
The striking feature of this campaign is the reduced
dominance of the two major parties. Across the major
polls, neither traditional centre-left or centre-right
blocs can be confident of governing alone, and the
composition of the minor-party vote will determine both
who governs and how. Because the Opportunity Party

sits near the threshold, a movement of only a few tenths
of a percentage point could alter the allocation of several
seats. In addition, any “overhang” created by Te Pāti Māori
or the National Party winning more electorates than their
party vote entitlement could also move the majority line
above the usual 61 seats.
ECONOMIC BULLETIN
Preview of 2026 General Election.
27 Oct 2026 |  Westpac Economics Team | westpac.co.nz/economics | economics@westpac.co.nz

Kelly Eckhold, Chief Economist | +64 9 348 9382 | +64 21 786 758 | kelly.eckhold@westpac.co.nz

### Page 2

2
Westpac New Zealand Economics
Together with uncertainty about voter turnout –
especially considering changes in voter registration rules
– the outcome of this General Election is difficult to call.
With major-party support unusually low and the minor
parties offering distinct, sometimes internally conflicting
programmes, the shape of the next government will be
determined not only at the ballot box on 7 November,
but at the negotiating table afterwards. Moreover, if the

results are especially tight, the outcome may not be clear
until sometime after the official results are declared on
27 November. And a hung parliament – where neither the
centre-left nor centre-right bloc can command a majority
– cannot be entirely ruled out either.
How New Zealand’s electoral system works.
New Zealand’s electoral system uses Mixed Member
Proportional representation (or MMP). Voters receive
two votes: an electorate vote for a local candidate and a

party vote for a political party. The electorate candidate
with the most votes wins the local seat. The party vote is
the main determinant of each qualifying party’s overall
representation in Parliament.
Parliament normally has 120 seats. For the 2026
election there are 71 electorates, comprising 64 general
electorates and seven Māori electorates, with the
remaining seats ordinarily filled from party lists. A party
qualifies for proportional representation by receiving

at least 5% of the nationwide party vote or winning an
electorate seat. List MPs are then added to electorate
MPs so that each qualifying party’s total representation
broadly matches its party-vote share.
An overhang arises when a party wins more electorate
seats than its party vote would otherwise entitle it to.
The party keeps those seats and Parliament expands,
which can raise the number required for a majority.
Government formation is separate from the allocation of

seats: a grouping must demonstrate that it can command
confidence in the House and pass supply. That can be
achieved through a formal coalition, a confidence-and-
supply agreement, or other support arrangements. The
largest party is not automatically guaranteed office and
minority governments are possible (e.g. although the
National Party secured more than 44% of the vote in the
2017 election, a minority Labour Party-NZ First coalition
was formed, supported by a confidence-and-supply

arrangement with the Green Party).
The overhang risk is unusually important in 2026 because
current polling leaves both blocs close to, but often
short of, a majority. In addition, the fragmentation of
the electorate increases the risk that one of the major
parties wins more electorates than implied by its party-
vote entitlement. This is in addition to the likelihood that
Te Pāti Māori will achieve an overhang given that it is
running an “electorate vote only” campaign and so asking

its supporters to cast their party votes for other left-
leaning parties. Each additional overhang seat expands
Parliament and so lifts the threshold needed to command
a majority.
The direction of the effect depends on which party
creates the overhang. Extra Te Pāti Māori electorate seats
would add MPs aligned more naturally with a Labour-
Green administration and could make Te Pāti Māori
support or abstention more consequential. A National
overhang would add seats on the right and could improve

the arithmetic for a National-ACT-NZ First government.
Under the latest RNZ-Reid Research projection, for
example, an overhang would arise if National won more
than 32 electorates or Te Pāti Māori won more than two.
This makes the party vote strategically important and
explains the focus on Opportunity’s threshold position.
A vote share just below 5% would produce no list seats
unless the party won an electorate (which based on
limited polling presently seems unlikely). A result just

above 5% would give it a parliamentary caucus large
enough, on current polling, to become the “kingmaker”
and decide which side can form a government. While
the Opportunity Party says that it could work with either
a centre-left or centre-right government, as detailed in
the appendix its broad policy platform might imply that it
more naturally aligns with the centre-left.
What the polls imply.
Kiwipoll’s six-poll tracker, last updated on 20 October,

put Labour on 27.9% and National on 28.7% - a gap
of just 0.8 percentage points. More strikingly, the two
major parties together attracted only 56.6%. The central
message across all polls is therefore fragmentation:
neither major party is close to governing alone, and almost
half of the represented vote is distributed among parties
that would shape the terms of any governing arrangement.
On that average, the balance between the established
blocs is also extremely narrow. The model associated

with the tracker assigned 56 seats to Labour, the
Greens and Te Pāti Māori, and 61 to National, ACT and
New Zealand First. Neither side reached a majority in a
presumed 124-seat parliament. Opportunity’s average
support of 6.2% translated into seven seats, enough to
take either bloc to the 63-seat threshold needed for a
majority. The average therefore implies that the identity
of the largest party may matter less than whether
Opportunity remains above 5% and which grouping it is

prepared to support.
This is not simply a close two-bloc contest. The polling
average also suggests that the minor parties would enter
negotiations with unusually large leverage. The Greens,
ACT and New Zealand First would consequently have
more scope than usual to influence tax, expenditure,
regulation, immigration and resource policy, or the
allocation of Cabinet positions, making it harder to
determine the likely form of a final coalition agreement.

### Page 3

3
Westpac New Zealand Economics
That said, the leverage that these parties can apply on
their major party coalition partner is limited. The Green
Party cannot credibly threaten that it would form an
alternative coalition with the National Party. And neither
could ACT or NZ First credibly threaten to form an
alternative coalition with the Labour Party (while NZ First
has worked with the Labour Party in the past, it has ruled
out doing so on this occasion). Moreover, it is possible

that minor parties would be punished by the electorate
if a failure to form a government necessitated a second
general election, especially if the minor parties were
perceived to have made unreasonable demands or if the
electorate simply refocused on establishing a workable
parliamentary majority.
The Opportunity Party is the pivotal uncertainty because
its average position is above the threshold, but not
far above it. A result modestly below 5%, without an

electorate victory, would remove its votes from the
seat allocation and substantially change the arithmetic,
favouring the centre-right bloc. A result around its recent
6% polling average (or higher) would make the party
central to government formation.
Formally, National has ruled out working with
Opportunity, due to its objection to the latter’s tax policy.
For its part, Opportunity has said that it would talk first to
the party with the most seats, which on present polling

could be either Labour or National. However, as noted
previously, given Opportunity’s policy orientation, it
seems more likely that it would ultimately join a centre-
left coalition than a centre-right coalition.
To summarise, the bloc contest is close, and the route
to a majority depends on threshold and electorate
outcomes that remain uncertain. With that in mind, we
think that the following two scenarios are the most likely
outcomes of the election.

Scenario one: a returned centre-right
government, with greater minor party
representation.
The first scenario is that the existing coalition is returned,
albeit with the minor parties gaining strength and thus
additional leeway to pursue their policy agendas. We
assume that the coalition is not dependent on the
Opportunity Party to govern, which would be more likely
to occur if the latter fell marginally short of the party
vote 5% threshold needed to enter parliament in the

first place.
A renewed National-led government would begin with
considerable continuity. National’s fiscal plan retains
the current coalition’s focus on restoring surplus,
reducing debt as a share of GDP , keeping new spending
constrained and making greater use of planning reform
and private capital. It projects an OBEGALx surplus of
$4 billion in 2028/29, with net core Crown debt peaking
at 43.9% of GDP in 2027/28 and falling to 39.5% by
2030/31. National has ruled out new tax cuts during the

next term, saying tax relief should wait until the books
are back in surplus and surpluses are growing. But a
coalition in which ACT and New Zealand First held a
larger share of seats would be different from the current
more National-dominated administration. Unless its vote
share is particularly low, National would almost certainly
still provide the Prime Minister (sharing the role with NZ
First and ACT in the context of a three-year electoral
term seems quite unlikely – the latter will likely share the

Deputy role as they have in the current term). The three
parties share a common fiscal framework at the macro
level. However, National would have less ability to defer or
dilute its partners’ priorities.
Fiscal policy and the size of government.
ACT would press for a faster and deeper reduction in
the state’s footprint. Its fiscal plan proposes cutting
$9.5bn across the forecast horizon from spending outside
welfare and protected frontline services, reducing future

operating allowances by $400m a year to $2.0bn, and
moving non-welfare spending back towards 2017 real per-
capita levels. ACT says this would lower debt by $12.4bn
by 2031 relative to current forecasts. It also proposes
consolidating government into 19 departments and
18 ministers.
On its own, that agenda would pull a renewed
government beyond National’s more incremental
expenditure control. Coalition negotiations would likely
turn on the pace of implementation and the definition

of protected frontline services. The economic effect
would depend on whether savings represented genuine
efficiency gains or reductions in service capacity and
transfers. In the near term, a faster consolidation could
subtract from demand, while lower debt issuance
and a smaller structural deficit would strengthen the
fiscal buffer.
However, New Zealand First would push in a different
direction. Its programme combines selective tax relief
with active government intervention, regional grants

and strategic ownership. The tension within the coalition
would therefore not simply be between ‘more’ and ‘less’
government. ACT would seek a smaller and more market-
oriented state; New Zealand First would accept a larger
role where it supported national ownership, regions,
families or strategic industries. New Zealand First’s
proposal to reduce the corporate tax rate for SME’s to
20% (from 28%) may well be accepted by its coalition
partners during coalition negotiations – possibly phased

in over time – and would certainly be much cheaper
than the fiscal cost of some other policies promoted
by the party (notably compensating employees for
higher compulsory Kiwisaver contributions and the
nationalisation of the Bank of New Zealand).

### Page 4

4
Westpac New Zealand Economics
Tax, welfare and retirement.
ACT’s longer-run objective is a lower and flatter tax
system. Its immediate plan removes tax on qualifying
KiwiSaver investment earnings, ends the government
KiwiSaver contribution and returns net Emissions Trading
Scheme auction revenue to households through a
carbon-tax refund. Broader tax relief is deferred until
the books are balanced. ACT would raise the NZ Super
eligibility age by three months a year to 67 by 2035, while

allowing compassionate early access for people with long
careers in physically demanding work. It would also pause
contributions to the NZ Super Fund.
On welfare, ACT proposes tighter medical certification
and reassessment for disability-related benefits, a five-
year stand-down before migrants can access welfare,
and lower spending outside protected services. NZ
First would add its own eligibility restrictions, including
limiting NZ Super to citizens from 2029, while proposing

compulsory KiwiSaver enrolment from birth and a Crown
contribution. These policies would create difficult design
questions around transitional arrangements, hardship
and administration, but the broad direction would be
towards tighter eligibility and stronger conditionality.
Regulation, resources and labour markets.
A stronger ACT-NZ First presence would reinforce
deregulation in planning, employment and resource
development. ACT would seek property-rights-based

planning law, easier dismissal, a three-year minimum
wage freeze, a lower training wage for workers under
20 and removal of Te Mana o te Wai from consenting.
It would repeal the Zero Carbon Act. National’s own
RMA replacement and fast-track agenda would provide
substantial common ground.
NZ First would push more directly for regional and
strategic development: a special economic zone at
Marsden Point, offshore oil and gas surveying, expanded
mineral development, and the return of 50% of mining

royalties to producing regions. It also proposes breaking
up the power gentailers and has advocated bringing a
major bank back into New Zealand public ownership.
Both the National Party and NZ First have policies that
aim to force additional competition in the supermarket
sector, but these are opposed by the ACT Party.
Nonetheless, the result could be a government that is
simultaneously more deregulatory in consenting and
more interventionist in ownership and competition policy.

Immigration would also tighten. ACT proposes annually
reviewed work-visa categories, a Rural Workforce Visa,
stronger overstayer enforcement and physical-presence
requirements for permanent residents. NZ First proposes
replacing the Accredited Employer Work Visa with a
shortage-led Skilled Work Visa and holding a referendum
on a 20,000 annual cap on resident visas. National’s more
skills-focused approach would be under pressure from
both partners to place greater weight on enforcement,

settlement obligations and lower aggregate inflows.
Scenario two: Labour and the Greens,
supported by Opportunity.
The second main scenario that we view as likely is a
Labour Party-led coalition with the Green Party, with the
Opportunity Party supporting either inside the coalition
or via a confidence-and-supply arrangement. Depending
on precise outcome of the election, a confidence-and-
supply arrangement may also be required with Te Pāti
Māori to secure the necessary parliamentary majority.

A Labour-led alternative would represent a clearer break
with the current policy direction, but the size of that break
would depend on the success of the minor partners in
embedding their policies in the coalition agreement.
For its part, the Labour Party would aim to restore and
expand selected public services while remaining inside
its published fiscal framework. Its plan retains a $2.4
billion operating allowance for each Budget, targets an
OBEGAL surplus in 2028/29 and seeks to reduce net

debt below 20% of GDP over time. It would introduce
a 28% capital gains tax on post-1 July 2027 gains from
investment residential and commercial property, with
major exemptions, and use the revenue to help fund
health initiatives.
Labour’s central commitments include free prescriptions,
three free doctors’ visits, additional screening and
maternity services, 6,000 additional state houses,
public-transport fare caps, expanded apprenticeships, a
care-worker pay increase and partial reversal of planned

public-service cuts.
It would also restore the Reserve Bank’s dual price-
stability and maximum-sustainable-employment
mandate. As we have discussed in detail in a previous
note, in the current environment – dominated by supply
shocks – the return of the dual mandate could mean that
the RBNZ is prepared to accommodate a slower return of
inflation to target to reduce the impact on employment.
The Greens would shift the centre of gravity.
A strong representation of Green Party ministers in

the Cabinet would seek to move a coalition further on
redistribution, public ownership, housing and climate
policy. The party’s tax programme includes a 2.5% wealth
tax on net assets above $10 million excluding the family
home, a capital-acquisitions tax above $1 million with
exemptions, a 45% top personal rate, a higher company
tax rate for the largest firms, and a levy on large banks.
The stated objective is to raise about $5.1 billion net and
shift the tax burden from wages towards wealth and

large corporations.

### Page 5

5
Westpac New Zealand Economics
These taxes are needed to finance the Green Party’s
extensive promises on spending (see below). Formally,
the Labour Party has explicitly ruled out supporting the
Green Party’s tax policies to secure a Labour Party-led
coalition. Whether that pre-election commitment would
be reversed after the election is a matter of conjecture.
On spending and ownership, the Green Party proposes
free dental care and public transport, stronger income

support, expanded school lunches, more public housing,
a publicly owned renewable-energy entity and the
acquisition of supermarket stores to establish KiwiMart.
A centre-left government, especially with strong Green
Party representation, may find it harder to resist pressure
to lift public service salaries and the minimum wage
in response to fuel-driven inflation. Relative to PREFU
forecasts, this would imply additional expenditure that
would need to be met with either additional sources of

revenue or additional debt issuance.
Labour would resist parts of this agenda on cost,
implementation risk and fiscal credibility. The most likely
coalition bargain would therefore be a narrower set of
high-priority Green Party reforms, financed by some
combination of new revenue, reprioritisation and the
available Budget allowances.
The regulatory direction would be unambiguously
stronger. Labour and the Greens overlap on restoring
pay equity (which was not explicitly budgeted for in

Labour’s fiscal plan), strengthening worker protections,
improving rental standards, expanding public housing
and using government more actively in markets with
weak competition. Climate, freshwater and biodiversity
rules would tighten relative to the current government,
and mining on conservation land would face much
greater barriers.
Opportunity’s role, potentially outside Cabinet.
Opportunity could support a Labour-Green government,
either directly joining the coalition or through a confidence-

and-supply agreement. While the former would give
Opportunity seats at the Cabinet table, the latter
arrangement would let the party retain a distinct identity,
trading reliable support on confidence and budgets for a
defined policy programme. It may also provide more scope
for the party to “learn the ropes” with less public scrutiny,
given that none of its prospective MPs have ever been in
Parliament, let alone held Cabinet positions.
Regardless of what form the arrangement takes,

Opportunity’s landmark policies are neither a simple
extension of Labour nor of the Greens. Opportunity
proposes a “tax reset”, including a land value tax, with a
lower rate for rural land, alongside personal income tax
rates of 28%, 34% and 39%. The revenue raised would be
used to fund a “Citizen’s Income” payment to almost all
people, which would also replace several current social
welfare benefits. That said, Opportunity has conceded
that the land tax and Citizen’s Income are unlikely to be

implemented in the next Parliament given that it will
probably be the smallest of the coalition partners.
In negotiations, Opportunity would press for the
inclusion of its five bottom lines. These are (1) giving
the Commerce Commission more teeth and greater
independence to tackle excessive market power in
supermarkets and other uncompetitive sectors, (2)
regulating lobbying and capping political donations,
(3) developing a 10-year plan to "fix the health system"

alongside health professionals, with clear commitments
on funding, workforce and infrastructure, (4) phasing
out bottom trawling, working with industry, iwi and
coastal communities to fairly manage the transition,
(5) recommitting to Paris Agreement emissions
reduction obligations and protecting the Climate Change
Commission's independence. Opportunity’s other policies
include allowing net debt to exceed 50% of GDP if the
additional borrowing finances productive infrastructure.

It also proposes compulsory KiwiSaver contributions
rising to 6% for employees and employers (a policy it
shares with National), free public transport, stronger
competition enforcement, higher R&D spending and a
ten-year cross-party education plan. In a confidence
and supply arrangement Opportunity could reserve the
right to oppose other legislation it disagrees with, but
the arrangement would need sufficiently clear fiscal
guardrails to avoid repeated uncertainty over Budgets

and concerns about the stability of the Government.
The economic stakes.
The two scenarios imply meaningfully different policy
mixes, but neither government would operate without
constraints. The current fiscal forecasts already assume
a return to surplus and a declining debt ratio later in the
forecast period. Delivery depends on tight expenditure
control, revenue growth and an economic outlook
exposed to oil prices, global interest rates and domestic

productivity. Coalition agreements that materially add
to operating spending or reduce revenue would need
offsetting measures if the party heading the coalition
wished to preserve its stated fiscal track.
For monetary policy, the direct institutional contrast is
Labour’s proposal to restore the Reserve Bank’s dual
mandate. In practice, the larger near-term influence may
come through fiscal demand, labour market regulation,
migration and energy policy. A faster fiscal consolidation

on the right would tend to reduce demand pressure, while
a larger operating spending programme on the left would
increase demand pressure. Infrastructure investment
is somewhat different: if well selected and delivered, it
can lift productive capacity over the medium term, but
it can also add to near-term capacity pressure before
supply benefits arrive. And given the current interest rate
environment, the hurdle that must be crossed to justify
raising debt is higher than it has been for years.

Source PDF: all report pdf/Quantum dictionary, part 1_ Decoding the fundamentals From superposition to decoherence, we unpack the concepts and technologies shaping the future of quantum computing. 2 min read.pdf
Report source: Bank of America
Date: 2026-10-01

Status: ok

### Page 1

13030420     1

Transformation 
Quantum dictionary, part 1: Decoding the fundamentals 
21 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• What is quantum computing? How does it work? What is a qubit? And when will quantum reach its full potential? Quantum 
computing challenges our intuition and our understanding of what computers can do. In this two-part dictionary series, we 
decode key terms behind what could be the next major technological revolution. 
• Qubits are the basic building blocks of quantum computers, harnessing properties such as superposition and entanglement to 
process information. However, qubits are highly sensitive to their surroundings where disturbances can lead to errors in 
quantum computation, making error correction a key challenge for building quantum computers. 
• There is no single approach to building a quantum computer. Researchers and companies are pursuing a range of qubit 
technologies, from neutral atoms and photons to spin, superconducting, topological and trapped-ion qubits, while quantum 
computing itself is likely to increasingly work alongside classical computing and AI.

First, let’s lay the groundwork… 
Quantum computing  
Quantum computing uses qubits and quantum properties such as superposition (defined later on) to perform complex 
calculations and solve certain problems faster than classical computers.1 From a practical perspective, quantum computing could 
be used to tackle problems related to optimization, machine learning, simulation and factorization across a range of sectors, 
including autos and logistics, financial services, energy and climate, health and life sciences and national security. 
Current quantum computers can perform ‘just’ thousands of quantum operations. As the technology advances from thousands to 
millions, billions and eventually trillions of operations, quantum computers could begin to have commercial applications.  
• Millions: Financial services and security could experience some of the first use cases, such as threat detection, fraud 
prevention, derivatives pricing, bond trading and arbitrage. 
• Billions: Quantum could help power AI through advanced data generation capabilities.  
• Trillions: Quantum chemistry applications could emerge, including drug discovery, battery simulation and materials 
discovery. 
These are the publicly known applications, according to Oxford Quantum Circuits (OQC). And as quantum computing capabilities 
improve, some of these use cases could emerge sooner than anticipated.  
Now, we’ll tackle the core concepts… 
Qubits 
Qubits (quantum bits) are a quantum computer’s basic unit of information. They can exhibit quantum properties such as 
superposition and entanglement (which we elaborate on below) — properties fundamental to quantum computing. 
Quantum gates manipulate the state of qubits, much like logic gates manipulate bits in classical computers. Despite the name, a 
quantum gate is not a physical object, but rather an operation applied to a quantum system that changes its state. 
From physical to logical qubits 
Physical qubits are the foundation of a quantum computer’s hardware. However, they are highly susceptible to error. To address 
this, multiple physical qubits can be structured together to encode a logical qubit, allowing errors to be detected and corrected

1 Schneider, J., & Smalley, I. (2025, June 10). What is quantum computing? IBM. 
I NSTI TUTE
Accessible version

### Page 2

2 21 October 2026 
  I NSTI TUTE

and making the stored quantum information more reliable. In recent years, according to BofA Global Research, logical qubits 
have become a greater focus for quantum computing companies. 
Entanglement 
Entanglement is a condition where two or more qubits become linked so that their states are correlated, regardless of distance 
(Exhibit 1). It allows quantum computers to process information in ways that can be difficult or impossible for classical 
computers. 
So, how does it work? Suppose we have two photons that have opposite polarizations (the orientation of their light waves). 
Before measurement, neither has a definite polarization. When one is measured as horizontally polarized, the other will be 
vertically polarized when measured in the same basis, wherever it is. When measured separately, they appear to be random, but 
when compared, they are correlated (Exhibit 2). 
The challenges 
Once created, entanglement must be protected from decoherence (where interactions with the environment destroy quantum 
correlations). Maintaining entanglement over long distances is another challenge. Photons traveling through optical fiber can be 
absorbed or scattered, making long-distance quantum communication difficult. 2 Quantum repeaters aim to overcome this 
challenge by extending entanglement across a series of shorter links. 
Exhibit 1: Entanglement is a state where at least two qubits become 
correlated 
An infographic of entangled qubits

Source: BofA Global Research 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Exhibit 2: Measuring one qubit in an entangled pair instantly 
determines the state of the other, no matter the distance 
An example of what entanglement means for qubit measurement

Source: BofA Global Research 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Superposition 
Unlike a classical bit, which can be one of two states (0 or 1), superposition allows a quantum system to exist in multiple states 
or configurations at the same time (Exhibit 3). For example, a qubit can exist in the 0 state, the 1 state or any state that is a 
combination of 0 and 1.3  
A quantum system can maintain a superposition until it is observed or measured. Once measured, its superposition collapses into 
a single state. Another way of thinking about superposition is to imagine a sphere with the north pole representing zero and the 
south pole representing one. Other points on the surface of the sphere represent different superposition states. This allows us 
to: 1) represent complex forms of information; and 2) simulate atoms in a computer. 
Schrödinger’s cat 
In 1935, physicist Erwin Schrödinger proposed a thought experiment to illustrate the strange implications of quantum 
mechanics. In this scenario, a cat, a radioactive substance that decays very slowly, and a Geiger counter are put together in a 
sealed box. According to quantum mechanics, the system can be described as a superposition in which the cat is both alive and 
dead until the box is opened. Once the box is opened and the system is observed, the superposition collapses into a single 
outcome.

2 Rehman, M. (2026, May 11). What is Quantum Entanglement? Quantum Insider. 
3 Microsoft. (n.d.). Superposition.

### Page 3

21 October 2026    3

I NSTI TUTE

Enabling parallel computation versus classical computing 
Superposition is a key attribute of quantum computing that allows for quantum parallelism, or the ability to perform multiple 
computations at the same time. Classical bits can only be in one of two states, 0 or 1. In contrast, a qubit can be in a 
superposition of 0 and 1, allowing a quantum computer to process multiple possible qubit states at once.4  
Exhibit 3: Superposition is the condition where a qubit can exist in a superposition of 0 and 1 
Illustrating the power of superposition

Source: BofA Global Research 
Note: A Bloch sphere is a geometric representation of qubit states. 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Error rates and fidelity 
Fidelity is a measure of how closely a quantum state or operation matches what was intended (Exhibit 4). A higher fidelity 
generally means fewer errors. 
Physical qubits are prone to errors. One way to address this is to combine multiple physical qubits to create a more reliable 
logical qubit. Quantum error correction (QEC) allows errors in the physical qubits to be detected and corrected, helping protect 
the information stored in the logical qubit.

4 Ibid.

### Page 4

4 21 October 2026 
  I NSTI TUTE

Exhibit 4: Fidelity measures how accurately a qubit retains its intended quantum state during computation 
Infographic showing the implications of high versus low fidelity

Source: BofA Global Research  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
How does quantum error correction work? 
At its simplest, QEC relies on redundancy, or storing information across multiple qubits so that errors can be detected and 
corrected.  
Consider an example: suppose someone wants to send a message represented by 0. Instead of sending a single 0, the message 
could be repeated three times as 000, known as a codeword. If one bit were accidentally flipped from 0 to 1 during transmission, 
the recipient might receive 010. Because two of the three bits are still 0, the recipient could infer that the intended message 
was 000. 
However, if two bits flipped, producing 110 or 011, the recipient could infer the wrong message. If all three flipped to 111, the 
recipient would have no way to know from the received codeword that errors had occurred.5 This approach is known as a 
repetition code and illustrates how redundancy can help protect information from errors.  
Repetition codes are a simple form of error correction that can address bit-flip errors, where a 0 changes to a 1 or vice versa. 
More sophisticated QEC codes are needed to address other quantum errors, such as phase flips.6 Shor’s code, developed by 
American theoretical computer scientist Peter Shor, uses nine physical qubits to encode one logical qubit and can correct one 
bit-flip error or one phase flip error, but it can’t correct both types of errors at the same time. Meanwhile, Steane’s seven-qubit 
code, developed by Oxford physicist Andrew Steane, can also correct both types of errors. It has the advantage of being fault-
tolerant, meaning the error-correction process itself does not introduce additional errors.7 
At what error level might we start seeing quantum advantage? 
Some classical computing systems today operate with extremely high reliability, often described as having "17 nines" of fidelity 
(99.99999999999999999%). By comparison, quantum computers currently achieve fidelity rates of around 99.9%. Gerald 
Mullally, CEO of OQC, has argued that quantum systems would need to add roughly three more nines of fidelity to achieve 
commercial relevance, or quantum advantage. 
Noise and decoherence 
Noise refers to unwanted disturbances that affect quantum systems and can lead to errors in computations (Exhibit 5). It can 
come from sources such as interactions with the environment, electromagnetic interference and temperature fluctuations. 
Coherence is a quantum system’s ability to maintain its quantum properties, including superposition and entanglement. When 
coherence is lost through interactions with the environment, the process is known as “decoherence,” causing the system to 
behave more classically (Exhibit 6).8

5 Microsoft Ignite. (n.d.) Introduction to quantum error correction. 
6 Ibid. 
7 Ibid. 
8 Quandela. (n.d.) Quantum Decoherence.

### Page 5

21 October 2026    5

I NSTI TUTE

To fully control qubits for calculations and information transfer, they need to be carefully protected from their surroundings. 
External factors like noise, energy, changes in Earth’s magnetic field, radiation from Wi-Fi or mobile phones or cosmic rays can 
interfere with the process. Even small disturbances can lead to decoherence, making it difficult to develop large-scale, fault-
tolerant quantum computers.9 
According to QuEra Computing, quantum noise can be mitigated through physical isolation of qubits, the development of more 
precise control techniques and quantum error correction, which helps detect and correct errors in physical qubits.  
Exhibit 5: Noise refers to unwanted disturbances, e.g., interactions 
with the environment, that lead to errors in quantum computation 
An infographic explaining quantum noise

Source: BofA Global Research 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Exhibit 6: Decoherence is when qubits lose their key quantum 
properties, superposition and entanglement 
An infographic of decoherence

Source: BofA Global Research 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Quantum processing unit (QPU) 
Referred to as the brain of the quantum computer, the QPU is the core hardware where qubits reside and where quantum 
computations take place. QPUs rely on quantum behaviors like superposition and entanglement to tackle problems in areas such 
as cryptography, simulation and optimization. Just as a CPU (central processing unit) in classical computing contains multiple 
components, a QPU includes quantum gates (operations that manipulate qubits in a controlled way, similar to logic gates in 
classical computing) and interconnects, which enable communication between qubits and other quantum processors.10 
Next, we’ll look at broader computing architecture… 
Heterogeneous computing  
Heterogeneous computing refers to a future computing landscape likely combining classical and quantum computing, potentially 
unlocking more powerful, faster and cheaper compute and helping solve problems that classical computers cannot. According to 
BofA Global Research, AI and quantum computing could also converge and complement each other, enabling more 
transformative use cases. 
Quantum modality  
A quantum modality refers to the type of physical technology used to create and manipulate qubits. We discuss the modalities in 
greater depth in the following section. 
Lastly, we outline qubit technologies… 
Neutral atom qubit 
A quantum modality that uses individual atoms with no electrical charge as qubits (Exhibit 7). The atoms are held in place using 
focused lasers known as optical tweezers. Neutral atom qubit systems do not require atoms to be ionized, which means fewer 
energy constraints and potentially simpler cooling requirements. Atoms such as rubidium or cesium are typically used. The qubits 
are manipulated by changing the energy levels or positions of the trapped atoms using laser pulses.

9 QuEra. (n.d.) Quantum Noise. 
10 SpinQ. (2025, January 16). What does a Quantum Computer Look Like? Explain Key Parts.

### Page 6

6 21 October 2026 
  I NSTI TUTE

Exhibit 7: A quantum modality where atoms with a net electrical charge of zero are the foundation for qubits 
Key features of neutral atoms

Source: BofA Global Research 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Photonic qubit 
A quantum modality that uses particles of light, known as photons, to carry quantum information (Exhibit 8).11 Optical 
components such as beam splitters, which divide light along different paths, and phase shifters, which change the phase of light, 
can be used to perform quantum operations. Photonic quantum computers can use integrated photonic chips to manipulate 
photons and enable quantum logic. 
Exhibit 8: A quantum modality relying on the quantum properties of light 
Key features of photonic qubits

Source: BofA Global Research 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

11 Moore, J. (2025, March 28). The 6 different types of quantum computing technology. TechTarget.

### Page 7

21 October 2026    7

I NSTI TUTE

Spin qubit 
A quantum modality that uses the intrinsic spin of electrons or nuclei in solid-state materials, such as silicon or diamond. These 
spins can be manipulated using magnetic or electric fields, and their states are read using optical or charge-sensing techniques. 
Spin qubits are particularly appealing because they can leverage existing CMOS (complementary metal-oxide semiconductor; a 
technology to build processors) manufacturing infrastructure, making it easier to integrate them with conventional processors. 
Superconducting qubit 
A quantum modality in which electronic circuits create qubits. They are built using Josephson junctions, where two 
superconductors are separated by a thin insulating layer. Microwave pulses manipulate the qubits’ quantum states to run 
computations.12  
Specifically, a resistance-free current oscillates back and forth around a quantum loop. An injected microwave signal excites the 
current into superposition states.13 To enable this process, superconducting materials are cooled to near absolute zero (around 
10-20 millikelvin) using dilution refrigerators, helping reduce thermal noise and maintain coherence. 
Topological qubit 
A quantum modality that aims to encode quantum information using topological properties rather than relying only on individual 
particle states (Exhibit 9). One way to think about this is through the paths certain quasiparticles take as they move around one 
another, creating “braids” in space-time. Because the information can depend on the overall pattern of these braids rather than 
small details of their paths, the approach is designed to be more resilient to certain disturbances. 
Exhibit 9: Resilient to small perturbations but requires special quasiparticles not yet been conclusively demonstrated in nature 
An infographic of topological qubits

Source: BofA Global Research 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Trapped-ion qubit  
A quantum modality in which ions (electrically charged atoms) are held in place using electromagnetic fields. These ions act as 
the qubits and are controlled by the system with tightly focused laser beams.14 The qubits are held inside ultra-high vacuum 
(UHV) chambers.

of pausing to assess the impact of OCR

Forecasts
OCR remains at 2.75%, further hikes remain
their base case.
Today the RBNZ left the OCR unchanged at 2.75%. This
was a shock to markets and most analysts as a 25bp hike
had been nearly fully priced by markets. The decision
went to a vote which resulted in a 4:3 split in favour of an
unchanged OCR.
The RBNZ has followed through on the pause it signalled
for this meeting back in the September Monetary Policy

Statement. All MPC members continue to see a case for a
higher OCR in time, but there are different views around
the timing of those increases. The Governor noted that
further interest rate increases remain likely in coming
meetings but are conditional on the whether the outlook
evolves in line with expectations.
The key case made by more hawkish MPC members
(Silk, Gourley and Conway) put greater weight on the
persistence of underlying inflation and the risk that higher

inflation becomes entrenched in price-setting behaviour.
They noted that core inflation has been above levels
consistent with the 2% target midpoint for some time,
28 Oct 2026 | Kelly Eckhold, Chief Economist | +64 9 348 9382 | +64 21 786 758 | kelly.eckhold@westpac.co.nz
ECONOMIC BULLETIN
RBNZ October 2026 Monetary Policy Review.

### Page 2

2
Westpac New Zealand Economics
and viewed aggregate economic growth as relatively
resilient despite weakness among many households and
businesses. In their view, tightening now would guard
against sustained inflation pressures and reduce the risk
of needing to do more later.
The case made by the majority in favour of no change
in the OCR (Breman, Hansen, Gai and Williams) argued
that the two recent OCR increases warranted more time
to assess their effects. They placed weight on inflation

having been weaker than expected in the Q3 data (the
Governor emphasised this point in answer to questions
at the press conference), smaller-than-anticipated
indirect effects from higher fuel prices, stable inflation
expectations and subdued wage growth. They also
emphasised downside risks to the recovery, particularly
the continued weakness in household consumption and
investment, and judged that there was little evidence
so far that businesses were using higher costs as an

opportunity to rebuild margins.
One point of note today is that the RBNZ seems
more concerned about risks to the global outlook,
notwithstanding its recent resilience – possibly
channelling some recent comments made by the IMF. The
RBNZ notes that geopolitical events, high energy prices,
and high and rising public and private debt levels pose
downside risk to global growth. It also notes uncertainty
about whether the surge in AI investment will be justified

by economic fundamentals. In addition, the MPC debated
whether higher global interest rates reflected a higher
neutral rate – due to higher inflation expectations and
demand for funding – or posed the risk of bringing
forward a potential global downturn (possibly leading to a
rapid decline in the event of an abrupt global adjustment).
The exchange rate has adjusted sharply lower today,
reflecting the market shock. The NZD/AUD exchange rate
has moved to a new cycle low of around 0.7980 at the

time of writing. This is in line with our year-end target for
the NZD/AUD exchange rate of 0.79 cents. Compounding
the downward pressure on the NZD/AUD exchange rate
has been strong CPI data in Australia combined with
hawkish RBA commentary. Our colleagues in Australia
continue to expect a Cash Rate hike in Australia on
Melbourne Cup Day and some risk of a further Cash
Rate rise in February. Hence downward pressure on the
NZD exchange rate seems a reasonable prospect given

interest rate differentials are weighing heavily on the NZD.
The RBNZ does not sound especially perturbed by the
prospect of weakness in the exchange rate and noted
that the low exchange rate is supportive of the medium-
term prospects of the export sector and the economy
more broadly.
Westpac outlook – December hike likely, but
not done deal.
We changed our forecasts from no change in the OCR
at the October meeting to a 25bp hike in the wake of the

CPI data last week. Given the absence of the hike today
we are reverting to the OCR forecast profile we had had
in place since July – i.e. one further 25bp hike in 2026
and then 25bp increases in the February and March
2027 meetings, followed by a slower pace of increases
at the June and September Monetary Policy Statement
meetings. Our forecast of a peak OCR of 4% from
September 2027 has remained unchanged throughout, as
has our assessment of a 3.75% neutral OCR.

That said, in the current uncertain environment, it goes
without saying that the evolution of monetary policy will
depend on how both global events and key data evolve
(we summarise the near-term economic diary below).
Therefore, as the RBNZ noted itself today, the exact timing
of the tightening profile is highly uncertain and even the
tightening we forecast at the December meeting should
not be regarded as a done deal.
Things to watch ahead of the 9 December

meeting.
The RBNZ’s next policy review is on 9 December, when it
will also publish a full Monetary Policy Statement (MPS)
and refreshed economic forecasts. How the RBNZ’s policy
stance evolves between now and then will depend on
both international and domestic factors.
Regarding the former, the MPC has clearly become
more concerned about risks to the global economic
outlook, stemming from geopolitical events, high energy
prices, high and rising public and private debt levels and

uncertainty about whether the surge in AI investment will
be justified by economic fundamentals. So, the RBNZ will
pay close attention to any developments that suggest
that downside risk to the global outlook is materialising.
There are only a few important domestic economic
indicators scheduled for release ahead of the 9 December
meeting (by contrast there is a full slate of indicators due
for release prior to the following meeting on 10 February).

We think the following are the key ones to watch.
• Q3 labour market surveys
(4 November): Indicators from these surveys will cast
light on economic activity in Q3, capacity pressures
in the labour market and whether the lift in headline
inflation is impacting growth in wages and salaries.
• Q4 RBNZ surveys
(17 November, 20 November, 24 November): Inflation
expectations measures from the RBNZ’s surveys of
professional forecasters, households and businesses

will be analysed to assess the impact of elevated
headline inflation.

### Page 3

3
Westpac New Zealand Economics
• October Selected Prices
(17 November): These pricing indicators will provide a
partial read on how inflation is tracking early in Q4.
• October/November ANZ Business Outlook
(29 October/30 November): The ANZ surveys will
provide a timely account of how business activity and
pricing behaviour is responding to the sharp rebound
in oil prices.
• October PMI and PSI surveys
(13 November/16 November): These may provide some

early insight regarding GDP growth in Q4.
In addition to the above, we will monitor a range of
other high-frequency indicators, such as monthly data
on filled jobs, consumer spending, building consents,
housing market activity and prices, job ads and consumer
confidence. We will also be paying close attention
to developments in prices for New Zealand’s key
export commodities.
Finally, with the General Election taking place on
7 November, it is possible that opinions regarding the

likely path of monetary policy could be impacted should
a Labour Party-led government – with an intention
to restore the RBNZ’s dual mandate – seem likely to
be formed.

Document 402: Swing State_ Navigating Midterm Volatility (2026-10-23)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Swing State_ Navigating Midterm Volatility (2026-10-23).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-10-23
Pages: 14

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

Swing State: Navigating 
Midterm Volatility

1 
Authors 
Laura Daniels

Josh Ray

Matthew Barnard

Parag Thatte

Brett Ryan 
Senior US Economist

October 23, 2026 
A client-facing version of this report is available at the Deutsche Bank Research 
Portal. The following Deutsche Bank Research analysts contributed to this report: 
Brett Ryan (econ), Binky Chadha and Parag Thatte (equity strategy), Benjamin 
Soff (data centers), Daniel Knauff (software), George Hill (healthcare), Omotayo 
Okusanya (REITs), Peter Abramowitz (office REITs), Mark DeVries (consumer and 
specialty finance), Nicole DeBlase (MI/EE), Corinne Blanchard (clean tech), 
Richa Harnain (transports), and Scott Deuschle (aerospace and defense).  
With the 2026 US midterm elections approaching, we look at key investor 
debates surrounding the event and legislative priorities of the next Congress. 
Despite the uncertainty that midterms can bring, the market appears fairly 
confident on expectations for the overall results. While a surprise Republican-
majority victory in both chambers is still possible, consensus is converging 
around current polling indicating Democrats are likely to flip the seats needed for 
a House majority and may win a Senate majority. Historically, an incumbent 
president has lost seats in 20 of the last 22 midterms. Investor expectations are 
in line with this. Polymarket, for example, is currently above 90% for Democrats 
gaining control of the House and above 60% for Democrats gaining control of the 
Senate.  
With this set of expectations, key investor debates instead focus on the 
legislative agenda for hot-ticket policy issues. Namely, these hinge on AI and 
data centers, affordability (affecting healthcare, housing, and education loans), 
and economic and fiscal policy. Debates also surround possible influence of the 
new Congress on the war in Iran and, by extension, energy prices. In this report, 
we cut through the noise on what the next Congress could reasonably advance 
from these agendas, what the impact would be on affected sectors, and how to 
address market uncertainty – and perceived certainty – into the elections. 
Historically, midterm elections have proven to be a check on administration 
policies. The number of seats lost in the midterms by the incumbent party is tied 
to the president’s approval rating going into midterms, which in turn tends to 
align with consumer confidence. President Trump’s ~38% approval rating, at a 
record low for his tenure, would suggest a little over a 45-seat loss for the 
Republican Party (in the House and Senate combined). That said, polling in the 
Senate races that will determine control of the chamber largely remains within 
the margin of error, and in 2024, Trump won in at least five of the seven core toss-
up states. Thus, the Senate remains a much closer call than the House race.

### Page 2

23 October 2026 
The Theme Screen

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 2

Figure 1: Presidential Approval vs. House Seats

Source: Real Clear Politics

Figure 2: Presidential Approval vs. Consumer Confidence

Source: The Conference Board

Whatever the outcome of the  midterms, the legislative agenda will 
still face constraints from the White House. It is all but impossible for either party 
to gain a veto -proof majority  in either chamber, minimizing the likelihood of a 
large shift in fiscal or regulatory policy. The most  immediately obvious impact of 
Democrats taking the Senate would be the ability to slow or block executive 
branch appointees, including at the Federal Reserve Board of Governors or the 
Supreme Court, should any members step down before their terms end.

### Page 3

23 October 2026 
The Theme Screen

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 3

Without garnering a two-thirds majority in both chambers, Democrats lack a clear 
path to unwind the 2025 tax cuts or enact AI-related regulatory legislation – at 
least, not without President Trump’s explicit approval. With respect to the near -
term fiscal policy outlook, the most important period for investors may be the 
“lame duck” session, the period after the November 3 election and before the new 
Congress convenes on January 3. Recall that current federal government funding 
expires on December 11, and the Trump administration is asking for a nearly 60% 
increase in defense spending, the largest since the Korean War.  In addition, the 
debt limit will become binding again around the middle of next year. In short, the 
actions during the lame duck session could determine whether the new Congress 
is seated amid a government shutdown, a contentious battle over the federal 
budget, and the debt limit looming in the background.  
In terms of the market reaction to midterm  elections, the risk-reward profile has 
typically been favorable , with 21 of the last 23 midterm years  seeing positive 
returns in the fourth quarter, with an average gain of 7% in the S&P. The gains are 
notable even after adjusting for typical positive year-end seasonality.

Figure 3: S&P Returns and Midterms

Source: Bloomberg Finance LP

It w ould not be unusual for the market to go sideways as investors hedge 
potential volatility and uncertainty, as we have seen previously ahead of other 
well-telegraphed pivotal events. This has historically been followed by a rally as 
the results became clear and  uncertainty dissipated, regardless of the result 
itself. In the current context, if election polls and prediction market probabilities 
continue being lopsided, the market may not treat the election as an uncertain 
risk event going in. Uncertainty could still ramp up after, and the typical midterm 
election rally may be pushed out. It is notable that the one prominent exception 
to the midterm rallies was in 2018 , when the market sold off sharply after the 
elections as uncertainty spiked around trade war 1.0 as well as Fed policy.

### Page 4

23 October 2026 
The Theme Screen

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 4

We note that the midterm elections also coincide with the third quarter earnings 
season, during which we expect companies to deliver growth of 34%. For next 
year, we expect growth to be a still -robust 17% with risks to the upside. 
Eventually, earnings tend to be the anchor for equities and have been for several 
years despite numerous domestic political as well as geopolitical shocks. 
Across sectors, most have rallied around past midterms in keeping with the 
market, but there isn’t a consistent pattern of out - or underperformance . This 
suggests that for the initial conditions for each sector, specific policies and 
positioning played a bigger role.  For that reason, we turn to a closer analysis of 
key legislative and policy debates impacting exposed sectors.

### Page 5

23 October 2026 
The Theme Screen

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 5

AI and Data Centers  
AI and data centers have become a campaign talking point for candidates from 
both the Democratic and Republican parties. This has spurred investor questions 
on whether to expect a national regulatory framework constraining data center 
buildouts. Some of th e headlines surrounding this debate overestimate what 
Congress can  accomplish through national legislation compared with what is 
possible at a state level, particularly during a presidential administration 
supportive of AI and data center acceleration. Still, the industry is responding to 
a political backdrop that is increasingly scrutinizing AI and data centers.  
The current administration has prioritized advancing AI and digital infrastructure 
development. The White House ’s AI Action Plan , released July 2025, included a 
set of executive orders supporting the rapid buildout of AI data centers by 
reducing certain federal regulatory and permitting burdens. The administration 
did not preempt state laws on data center infrastructure development and 
federal permitting orders generally do not override state zoning and utility rules. 
In March 2026 , the White House announced the Ratepayer Protection Pledge, 
under which large data center operators would voluntarily commit to covering 
the cost to upgrade generation, transmission, substations, or other necessary 
infrastructure to service these developments.  The industry’s response to these 
proposals has been largely constructive. The Data Center Coalition  (DCC), an 
industry group representing major  hyperscalers and data center  owners and 
operators came out in support of the initial version of the Pledge, while arguing 
that large -load customers across all industries also should cover the costs to 
upgrade energy infrastructure. According to the DCC , “the data center 
industry remains committed to being responsible partners where we  operate, 
which includes  paying our full energy costs  and investing in local jobs and 
workforce development.” 
Congress voted in September 2026 to codify the principles present in the Pledge 
via the Ratepayer Protection Act. The bill, which only required states to “consider” 
these standards, passed overwhelmingly in the House (417-3), but failed to reach 
60 votes in the Senate (57 -43). Critics noted the bill stopped short of imposing 
mandatory nationwide rules. 
Looking forward, we expect a Congress that is more focused on consumer 
protection might look to codify a more stringent version of these rules, requiring 
large-load customers to bear the full incremental costs associated with 
generation, transmission, and distribution upgrades.  However, even if such a 
framework were passed into law, we believe the overall economic impact to large 
data center operators would be relatively modest, for two reasons. 
For one, the White House would maintain a veto over  bills that include language 
it views as overly burdensome towards AI infrastructure development . Two, data 
center operators  generally underwrite projects only when they have a high 
degree of confidence in securing power and, where necessary, may already be 
embedding the cost of upgrading energy infrastructure into their investment 
plans. We expect developers to be capable of passing on sustained increases in 
power infrastructure costs to their customers , which, for many 100  MW+ 
developments, are the major investment-grade hyperscalers.

### Page 6

23 October 2026 
The Theme Screen

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 6

Though data center development is receiving increasing national attention, it is 
still largely a local issue, and the most consequential elections will be at the state 
and local levels. We have seen a number of state -driven actions on data center s 
in recent months, including: 
• In July, New York State’s governor issued an executive order to establish 
a temporary moratorium on data center development  while the state 
develops a standardized framework of rules for key issues (including 
responsibilities for power and energy infrastructure upgrades; additional 
power generation; clean energy; water use, emission, and waste 
management; and state/local financ ial incentives). The New York 
legislature also passed a one -year moratorium on Department of 
Environmental Conservation permits and approvals for 20  MW+ data 
center developments. 
• In August, Texas’ governor directed the state’s public utility commission 
to conduct a comprehensive audit of every data center advancing 
through the ERCOT (Texas’s grid operator) interconnection process. The 
audit will cover several notable areas including public financial support, 
electricity demand and supply, water use, community impacts, and 
ownership and control. 
• In September, California’s governor passed a comprehensive regulatory 
package, covering electricity, water, reporting, and environmental 
review. Most of these provisions take effect January 1, 2027, and phase 
in through 2027 -2028, but the package does not ban data centers or 
impose a statewide moratorium on development activity. 
• Oregon, Pennsylvania, New Jersey, and other states have also 
established orders to create standards and frameworks governing data 
center development (but without imposing broad moratoriums). 
At the more local level, a large number of moratoriums have been passed on new 
data center development. Data collected by SemiAnalysis, an independent 
research firm, shows that, as of September, more than 300 local data  center 
moratoriums have been adopted nationwide across counties, municipalities, 
townships, and other local governments.  
However, these state and local moratoriums appear  thus far to be less impactful 
to near- and medium-term development activity than headline coverage might 
otherwise suggest. According to SemiAnalysis, only about 2.3 GW of the 38 GW 
of data center capacity planned or under construction for 2027 has faced delays 
as a result of  the New York state executive order (0.8 GW) and/or local 
moratoriums (1.5 GW). 
We believe the primary reasons that moratoriums are, in reality, less likely to 
impact near -term development activity are (1) that these moratoriums largely 
impact new projects and applications (rather than existing developments in  
progress), (2) there may be flexibility to relocate or rezone early-stage projects in 
response to moratoriums, and (3) data center developers are increasingly 
partnering with local communities to gain their support via investments and other 
programs (as we wrote about recently).  Potential regulatory risk has not yet 
slowed capex spending patterns, with capex spend still expected to climb from

### Page 7

23 October 2026 
The Theme Screen

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 7

$0.5 trillion in 2025 to $1.3 trillion by 2027; data centers typically represent ~20-
30% of the total bill of materials for new AI capacity.  
Although the practical impact on near- and medium-term development pipelines 
appears less pronounced than headlines might suggest, all this is not to say that 
data centers will not face increasing local opposition following the midterms. 
Indeed, i n our conversations with industry participants, there is an 
acknowledgment that greater public scrutiny of data centers is not going away.  
Additional moratoriums, zoning restrictions, permitting requirements, or utility -
related conditions could affect the pace, cost , and overall feasibility of future 
projects.

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 8

Affordability Agenda: Healthcare, Housing, and 
Education Loans  
The affordability agenda has become a centerpiece of many Democratic as well 
as Republican campaign platforms.  This platform is impacting investor 
debates across healthcare, housing, and student loans. In healthcare,  the push 
among some Democrats for expanded health coverage under the Medicare for All 
banner is placing pressure on managed care stocks, but meanwhile, companies 
with exposure to Medicaid and  the Affordable Care Act (ACA) could see a 
supportive political backdrop. On housing, investor debates on the impacts of the 
affordability agenda have continued since passage of legislation in July 2026 that 
caps the number of single -family homes for -profit institutional investors can 
purchase. Affordability of higher education is likewise part of the conversatio n, 
leading investors to debate which levers Congress could pull that would affect 
student loan companies.  
Healthcare 
In healthcare, w e see the upcoming midterm elections as a sentiment event for 
managed care stocks , not a policy event.  With the presidential veto in place 
through 2028, we do not expect the government to enact direct healthcare 
legislation during the next Congress. Forecasts place the probability of a change 
in House control at roughly 79%, with both chambers changing in about 44% of 
simulations. Although investor skittishness around Medicare for All (M4A) rhetoric 
is high, we see the likelihood of M4A replacing private coverage as a near -zero 
probability in this timeframe.  
M4A has no realistic legislative path before 2029, but the rhetoric weighs on 
managed care organization (MCO) stock multiples by raising perceived long-term 
risk rather than affecting near -term earnings. Polling from  Kaiser Family 
Foundation, a health policy research organization, shows support for M4A at a 
decade-plus high, and the 2018 –2019 cycle suggests that increased campaign 
attention can pressure MCO multiples even without a direct policy impact. We 
expect similar sentiment -driven pressure through year -end and potentially into 
the 2028 primary cycle but would not view it as a change i n near -term 
fundamentals.  
For Medicare Advantage, we see a h eadline risk over earnings reality. The near-
term earnings impact for affected companies should be limited, because the US 
Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) has finalized a 2.48% Medicare 
Advantage (MA) rate increase for 2027, versus 0.09% in the advance notice. A 
change in House control would instead increase focus on 2028 payment policy, 
risk adj ustment, and prior authorization. Coding reform already has bipartisan 
support: sponsors estimate that the Cassidy -Merkley No UPCODE Act could 
generate up to $275  billion of savings over ten years, while MedPAC estimates 
coding intensity will add roughly $40  billion to 2025 Medicare Advantage  costs 
relative to traditional Medicare. In our view, the more immediate consideration is 
potential multiple pressure rather than a measurable change to company 
earnings.  
However, the next Congress could b enefit Medicaid funding and ACA policy. 
Medicaid and the ACA appear relatively better positioned, although any benefit

### Page 9

23 October 2026 
The Theme Screen

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 9

would likely be reflected first in sentiment and lower perceived policy risk rather 
than near -term estimate revisions. Medicaid community engagement 
requirements take effect in most states by January 1, 2027. Although repeal 
remains unlikely given the presidential veto, a change in House control could shift 
the year -end funding debate toward  restoring or  preserving federal Medicaid 
support, which could ease state budget pressure and support rate -setting. The 
ACA policy backdrop could also become more supportive, although we see 
limited scope for enhanced premium subsidies to return before 2029 after the 
three-year extension passed by the House stalled.  
Housing 
Turning to the housing sector, the likely midterm outcome of a split government 
would result in more gridlock. Historically, political gridlock in terms of passing 
new regulation has been a tailwind for the real estate sector, reducing regulatory 
risk at the federal level. In the scenario of a split Congress, in which the Democrats 
have a majority in the House but not the Senate, bills with bipartisan support have 
a greater likelihood to become law. Two key areas in which this bipartisan support 
exists are housing affordability and consumer protection. 
Among real estate investment trusts ( REITs), we see Residential as among the 
most impacted subsectors, particularly as it relates to the affordability debate. 
Regardless of the election outcome, housing affordability has become front and 
center on the political agenda  across both parties. F ederal legislation  is more 
likely to target supply -side solutions, as legislation restricting rent increases is 
typically handled at the local and state level. We think Multifamily would be the 
most likely subsector impacted, as a push to expand rental housing supply could 
lead to eased zoning restrictions, particularly in supply -constrained coastal 
markets that have historically benefited from higher barriers to entry. That said, 
this would likely take years to materialize. With a Democratic majority , Congress 
could voice opposition to the influence of large institutional landlords, but we see 
this strictly as a sentiment risk to stocks.  
Single Family For Rent (SFR) is less exposed to near -term legislative shocks, as 
the sector was a focus of the 21st Century ROAD to Housing Act passed in July 
2026 over the summer. The  measure impacts institutional owners’ ability to buy 
homes off the MLS but contains specific carve-out provisions that will allow them 
to keep developing build -to-rent (BTR) product and buy excess inventory from 
homebuilders, the sector's preferred acquis ition channel in recent years. The 
ROAD to Housing Act also contained provisions that could theoretically ease the 
production of manufactured homes, the main business segment for 
Manufactured Housing (MH) REITs. The first-order impact is likely more flexibility 
in home designs that could lead to further densification of MH communities, 
although we note emerging investor debate about the long -term impacts of 
increased MH production on competitive supply for a property type t hat has 
historically been supply constrained, mainly due to NIMBY-ism.  
Parallel to the conversation on affordability, it is clear that, regardless of which 
political party may be in power following the midterms, a major focus remains 
addressing the US fiscal deficit and its continued growth. We continue to pay 
attention to potential budget cuts or sequestration to slow government spending 
and reduce the deficit, which could have an impact on real estate.  Key areas to 
watch include cuts to healthcare spending (which would be a negative for skilled

### Page 10

23 October 2026 
The Theme Screen

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 10

nursing focused REITs, given Medicare is a major revenue source for their tenant 
base) and cuts that would impact Office REITs with large government exposure.  
We also worry that an elevated federal deficit and the elevated Treasury 
borrowing needs today create a risk that interest rates will remain  higher for 
longer. This puts pressure on real estate valuations and cost of financing for REITs 
to drive external growth via acquisitions and development. A split Congress is 
likely to result in more difficult budget negotiations and increased oversight, but 
it remains unclear w hat the ultimate outcome would be to address the budget 
deficit issue. 
With real estate being more dependent on local market dynamics, we believe the 
more likely regulatory impact would come from local regulation. This could show 
up on the ballots during the midterms , with increased possibility of rent control 
legislation targeted at residential real estate as well as self -storage, as cities and 
municipalities focus on affordability issues. There is also growing legislative 
pushback at the local level against development of real assets such as data 
centers given the large power requirements, as discussed in detail elsewhere in 
this report.  
Education Lending 
Midterm campaign platforms on education affordability  have a bearing on the 
consumer and specialty finance sector. Much of the policy exposure in this sector 
is through the executive branch bodies, not Congress, however. This includes, for 
example, regulatory authorities  such as the FDIC (Federal Deposit Insurance 
Corporation), the CFPB (Consumer Financial Protection Bureau ), or the FHFA 
(Federal Housing Finance Agency, which regulates Fannie Mae and Freddie 
Mac). That said, historical data shows that student loan company stocks can still 
directionally align with midterm party outcome and expectations  in Congress, 
although the sample size is small.
